## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

},\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/routes.tsx\\",\\r\\n        \\"kind\\": \\"file\\",\\r\\n        \\"sha256\\": \\"6477a58c2cfad95f5450a0c4bad585396100638ffe76647ca3eb9eefc52e2e51\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/useConditionalAssignments.ts\\",\\r\\n   \\"kind\\": \\"file\\",\\r\\n        \\"sha256\\": \\"10c903b7a3533499971e332f577dbd62e0c015bb7300ad51303ad9dcae4a9928\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"path\\": \\"src/components/teacher/ConditionalAssignmentFields.tsx\\",\\r\\n        \\"kind\\"…14520 tokens truncated…are requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\\r\\n| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\\r\\n| **Group 1** | Joshua\'s Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\\r\\n| **Group 2** | Joshua\'s Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression\'s target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua\'s assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give an idea several teachable moments\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve the real indicator groups\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current reviewed opening materials use:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n\\r\\nSee the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source\'s two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua\'s notes and scope.\\r\\n\\r\\n“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve Joshua\'s five steps\\r\\n\\r\\nIn [Sandra\'s 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua\'s five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Carry these improvements forward\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\\r\\n2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\\r\\n3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\\r\\n4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\\r\\n5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\\r\\n6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\\r\\n7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\\r\\n8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\\r\\n9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\\r\\n10. **Keep later content in its proper lesson.** Contraposition, chains, Groups 3/4, and quantities receive their own development; a quality reference does not override prerequisites.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\\r\\n| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\\r\\n| The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\\r\\n| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\\r\\n| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\\r\\n| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua\'s clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\\r\\n| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\\r\\n| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\\r\\n| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence\'s subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\\r\\n| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\\r\\n| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author\'s advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |\\r\\n| References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, or named source tools. | Preserve the teaching function only where our course has an actual equivalent. Do not import obsolete or unavailable context. |\\r\\n| A theme makes a drill memorable. | Keep each sentence logically interpretable and its answer reviewed. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.\\r\\n- Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.\\r\\n- Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces\\r\\n\\r\\nAll examples below are original drafts for this reference. They demonstrate writing mechanisms; they are not assigned work or approved curriculum.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain sufficient and necessary without rushing\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose yours has a blue sticker. That\'s enough to know it has been inspected. You don\'t need another fact about the parcel. The rule has already told you what must be true of every parcel with that sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We call the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We still don\'t know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning\\r\\n\\r\\n> Editing this newsletter requires an account.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Front**\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Back**\\r\\n\\r\\n> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n\\r\\n> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n\\r\\n> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n>\\r\\n> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n\\r\\n### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n\\r\\nList what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n\\r\\nRead as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n\\r\\nPut the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nRead aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n\\r\\nScore each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nWrite this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\nRead wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\nand wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\nthe idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\nShow each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\nwould actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\nroom for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nVary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\nsentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\nTeach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\nRepeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\nShow a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\nand the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\nDo not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\ncounts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\nor decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\nprompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\nfollows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keep this reference current\\r\\n\\r\\nThis file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua\'s later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source archive is immutable. Future evidence or revised attachments should be recorded as a separate generation with provenance, not written over the original.\\r\\n"}}\n{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"6fdf42","wall_time_seconds":4.343444,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":0,"output":""}}'}]

Two arrowheads meeting at a result do not provide a forward path from one source to the other.\' },\\r\\n"}}\n{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"772989","wall_time_seconds":1.8436037,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7114,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its tar…154 tokens truncated… Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |\\r\\n| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that\'s the rule.” |\\r\\n| Inviting attention | let\'s look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |\\r\\n| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |\\r\\n| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |\\r\\n| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntroduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nA repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition\'s shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.\\r\\n\\r\\nTransfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader\'s desire for certainty a recognizable shape.\\r\\n\\r\\nHumor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua\'s source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source\'s distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.\\r\\n\\r\\nNever turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua\'s biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision\\r\\n\\r\\nBold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.\\r\\n- Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.\\r\\n- Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.\\r\\n- Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.\\r\\n- Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.\\r\\n- Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.\\r\\n- Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The complete source progression and depth\\r\\n\\r\\nThis map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated\\r\\n\\r\\n**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.\\r\\n"}}'}]

When the saved cursor points to an ID outside the current filtered questions, the new branch (`if (!question && !run.finished)`) only shows \'This saved question is not available in this assignment...\' and a \'Return to your work\' link. That link leads back to the same practice. routes.test \'keeps a current question outside the selected assignment without offering a destructive reset\' locks in this behavior. `advance` makes it worse: nextUncheckedQuestionIndex scans run.questionIds rather than the current `questions`, so a run built under a broader assignment can move into an unavailable ID partway through and never reach \'Finish set\'. Scenario: a translation-area run built from group-1 and group-2 items is opened under an assignment whose allowedUnits is [\'group-2\']. The student cannot practice the assigned work at all. Counters (`run.index + 1 / run.questionIds.length`) also count items the student cannot open.\\",\\r\\n      \\"fix\\": \\"Keep the old run intact, but make the cursor and `advance` skip IDs not in the current `questions`. Compute progress counts over the available intersection. When none of the current items remain, offer a non-destructive \'Start this assignment\'s set\' that creates a new run under an assignment-scoped key, for example by appending the assignment ID. Leave the existing run and its attempts unchanged. Add a route test showing a student completing the assigned subset.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"id\\": \\"CI3-2\\",\\r\\n      \\"severity\\": \\"medium\\",\\r\\n      \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/content/foundations.source.ts\\",\\r\\n      \\"evidence\\": \\"Some reasoningSteps answer the hint question, not the item. They are joined into `explanation` and shown after \'Answer:\' in QuestionRunner and in Diagnostics review, where no hint is shown.\\\\n- orientation-m1 (line 60): the answer is \'No; it remains unsettled.\' and the first step reads \'Yes. Most inspected lifts can operate quietly...\'. \'Yes.\' is also wrong option A, so a student who chose A sees their wrong answer apparently confirmed.\\\\n- sufficient-necessary-m2 (line 139): the answer is \'Unknown.\' and the first step reads \'Yes. Tagged specimens must be stored...\'.\\\\n- group-1-m5 (line 287): the answer is \'Its ending is not established.\' and the first step reads \'Yes. When the rehearsal ends...\'.\\\\nThis breaks the guide\'s \'Make feedback identify the missed connection\' and \'Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence\'. orientation-m1 also assesses \'most\', which the lesson never taught; it taught \'usually\'.\\",\\r\\n      \\"fix\\": \\"Rewrite each first step as a direct statement that does not depend on the hint. For example: \'An inspected lift could be noisy without contradicting most, so quiet operation isn\'t guaranteed.\' Consider \'usually\' in place of \'most\' in orientation-m1. Add a test that no reasoningSteps[0] begins with a bare Yes or No unless that matches the keyed option.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"id\\": \\"CI3-3\\",\\r\\n      \\"severity\\": \\"medium\\",\\r\\n      \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/opening.css\\",\\r\\n      \\"evidence\\": \\"`@media(max-width:680px){.co-arrow{flex-direction:column}.co-arrow>b{transform:rotate(90deg)}}` turns every diagram vertical on phones. The copy still says \'Place the target right\' and \'Place the target left\' (TranslationLab), \'Choose the one that belongs on the left. The remaining idea goes right.\' (DiagramBuilder), \'Goes left\' and \'Goes right\' (role pair), and \'Read it from left to right\'. mobile-group-2-method-final.png shows the \'Place the target right\' button above a vertical Admitted ↓ Member diagram. The plan explicitly excludes unexplained left/right instructions and requires readable phone diagrams.\\",\\r\\n      \\"fix\\": \\"Keep the arrow horizontal on narrow screens, letting the labels wrap with a smaller font; 375px can hold two short labels. Alternatively, label the vertical slots \'If (left side)\' and \'then (right side)\'. This is polish, not a blocker.\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"id\\": \\"CI3-5\\",\\r\\n      \\"severity\\": \\"low\\",\\r\\n      \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/content/foundations.source.ts\\",\\r\\n      \\"evidence\\": \\"Group 1 commonMistake.repair (line 271) reads: \'A report appears when a rover crosses the ridge starts with the report, but the crossing is what guarantees it.\' The embedded sentence has no quotation marks, so the line is hard to parse; desktop-repair-final.png shows it in the \'Read the sentence again\' card. The rover example appears only in the guided example, never in the lesson the student just read. The same text appears in the Reference dialog (routes.tsx:168).\\",\\r\\n      \\"fix\\": \\"Set the sentence apart with quotation marks or italics. Better, reuse the lesson\'s own if-clause anchor: \'In “A report is reviewed if it is submitted before noon,” review comes first, but submission before noon is what guarantees it.\'\\"\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      \\"id\\": \\"CI3-6\\",\\r\\n      \\"severity\\": \\"low\\",\\r\\n      \\"path\\": \\"src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx\\",\\r\\n      \\"evidence\\": \\"Small slips in logical strength and wording:\\\\n- Line 676, \'Why learn this?\': \'Another statement might allow that. The guide says: \'Avoid changing a logical could into must\' and \'be certain about the limits of what is known\'.\\\\n- Line 89: \'This sentence gives a ring whenever the kitchen timer reaches zero\'. The countdown reaches zero, not the timer.\\\\n- Lines 60 and 72 use stiff register (\'Under this stated rule, that event is enough for evacuation\'; \'an exceptionless result of being a finalist\'), which the guide warns against when plain speech would teach better.\\",\\r\\n      \\"fix\\": \\"Change the line 676 wording to \'But this rule alone doesn\'t.\' Correct the subject to \'its countdown reaches zero\'. The sentence ...\').\\\\n- Each group lesson has two near-identical recall decks: Flashcards under \'Say the rule without the scaffold\' and ConditionalOpeningMedia under \'Return to the relationship\'.\\\\nThe guide says to remove repeated catchphrases and not to render the same instructions twice in different panels. Strengths:\\\\n- \'Define before demonstrating\' and \'Technical names arrive at the useful moment\' are well applied. Orientation defines \'condition\' and \'conditional statement\' before the order rule; the arrow arrives only after \'Now that we understand the sentence\'; \'Personalized If/Then\' and \'What Test\' are named after the learner has used them.\\\\n- \'Confidence lives in the reasoning\' and \'We don\'t know\' are applied: the Order B pause gives a bold \'We don\'t know.\' and then explains the missing connection.\\\\n- \'Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\' and \'Contractions are normal\' are visible: \'Usually leaves room for exceptions... The second doesn\'t leave that possibility open.\'\\\\n- \'Make feedback identify the missed connection\' is met in the lesson Checks, for example \'You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist...\'.\\\\nWeaknesses: repeated boilerplate and templates (CI3-7), a hedge on logical strength (CI3-6), and drill explanations that open with a contradictory Yes or No (CI3-2). Overall the voice is close to the standard; I would approve it after CI3-2, CI3-6 and CI3-7.\\",\\r\\n    \\"(b) Learning progression and depth: strong. Orientation goes meaning → why it matters on the LSAT → tendency vs guarantee → rule in English → fact application → arrow (not causation or time) → changed fact → set explorer → fresh check. Sufficient-necessary has separate beats: enough; enough is not the only way; required; a requirement is not a guarantee; Personalized If/Then; roles belong to the rule. Each group has an intro defining indicator and target, then seven expression sections, each with a pause and readback. Those sections cover clause order (if, when, any, requires, only when), limiting modifiers, nonconditional inquiry and occasion uses with option-specific Checks, only vs the only in both groups, and must as an obligation. They are followed by the What Test lab, a whole-condition check, the diagram builder, recall, a meaning check, and a transition to guided practice and the drill. Scope stops at Group 2, and opening-coverage tests guard against contraposition and unless. The new drills are logically correct apart from the CI3-2 explanation wording.\\",\\r\\n    \\"(c) Visual design. Images I actually opened: additional-review/desktop-orientation, mobile-orientation, desktop-sufficient-necessary, mobile-sufficient-necessary, desktop-group-1, mobile-group-1, desktop-group-2 and mobile-group-2; plus desktop-personalized-method, desktop-what-test-final, desktop-repair-final, desktop-group-2-method-final, desktop-order-alternative-final and mobile-group-2-method-final.\\\\nWhat works: serif display type over a humanist sans body, readable line length, generous whitespace, calm green and cream colors, clear pause, try-it and lab cards, and an order explorer that shows containment and an allowed outside placement clearly with a caption about possibility. On phones the text is readable.\\\\nWhat falls short: the same hero in three units, a cropped orbit on phones (CI3-4), the vertical phone arrow conflicting with left/right copy (CI3-3), and a body that is mostly stacked rounded cards. Group lessons have no group-specific drawing beyond the arrow (CI3-9). The design is good but not yet \'memorable and varied\'.\\",\\r\\n    \\"(d) Interaction and platform integrity. Verified in source:\\\\n- Edition key only via markConditionalLessonRead on click, and excluded from lesson counts.\\\\n- Reviewed state recalculated for old readers.\\\\n- Expanded-lesson links hidden for practice-only work and gated by assignment.\\\\n- Reconciliation adds registered IDs only for represented unit and difficulty, removes retired IDs only when the replacement is allowed, keeps drafts and attempts, and preserves the cursor when the current item still exists.\\\\n- Historical resolver used only in Diagnostics review.\\\\n- Generated titles and descriptions normalized without rewriting stored rows.\\\\n- Legacy stage membership preserved, with v2 stage grants versioned.\\\\n- DEV preview route gated by import.meta.env.DEV.\\\\n- Lesson state resets per unit via key.\\\\n- Keyboard: all controls are native buttons with aria-pressed or aria-expanded; reduced-motion and pause CSS present; SVG aria-labels describe state.\\\\nOutstanding: the shared-run dead end (CI3-1). 0)).toBeGreaterThanOrEqual(270);\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'routes full-course assignment to the progressive path\', async () => {\\r\\n    expect(await resolveConditionalAssignment()).toMatchObject({ route: \'/bootcamp/conditionals\', key: \'course\', target: DEFAULT_CONDITIONAL_TARGET });\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'assigns a curriculum starting stage without a global activity level\', async () => {\\r\\n    const assigned = await resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'stage\', unitId: \'group-3\', mode: \'practice\', difficulty: \'advanced\' });\\r\\n    expect(assigned.route).toBe(\'/bootcamp/conditionals?start=group-3\');\\r\\n    expect(assigned.key).toBe(\'stage:group-3\');\\r\\n    expect(assigned.target).toEqual({ scope: \'stage\', unitId: \'group-3\', mode: \'learn\', difficulty: \'beginner\' });\\r\\n    expect(conditionalTargetKey({ scope: \'stage\', unitId: \'group-3\', mode: \'learn\', difficulty: \'medium\' })).toBe(assigned.key);\\r\\n    await expect(resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'stage\', unitId: \'missing\', mode: \'learn\', difficulty: \'beginner\' })).rejects.toThrow(\'existing conditional starting stage\');\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'routes a skill to learning or selected-level practice without changing the unit identity\', async () => {\\r\\n    const target = { scope: \'skill\', unitId: \'group-3\', mode: \'practice\', difficulty: \'advanced\' } as const;\\r\\n    const assigned = await resolveConditionalAssignment(target);\\r\\n    expect(assigned.route).toBe(\'/bootcamp/conditionals/practice?level=advanced&unit=group-3\');\\r\\n    expect(assigned.target).toEqual(target);\\r\\n    expect(assigned.title).not.toMatch(/Level \\\\d/);\\r\\n    expect(assigned.description).not.toMatch(/level \\\\d/i);\\r\\n    expect((await resolveConditionalAssignment({ ...target, mode: \'learn\' })).route).toContain(\'/lessons/group-3?level=advanced\');\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'preserves the teaching-order version in new stage grants and distinguishes legacy duplicates\', async () => {\\r\\n    const target = { scope: \'stage\', unitId: \'group-1\', mode: \'learn\', difficulty: \'beginner\', curriculumVersion: 2 } as const;\\r\\n    const assigned = await resolveConditionalAssignment(target);\\r\\n    expect(assigned.target).toEqual(target);\\r\\n    expect(assigned.key).toBe(\'stage:v2:group-1\');\\r\\n    expect(assigned.key).not.toBe(conditionalTargetKey({ ...target, curriculumVersion: undefined }));\\r\\n    expect(assigned.route).toBe(\'/bootcamp/conditionals?start=group-1\');\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'keeps general work conditional and permits independent quantifier area assignment\', async () => {\\r\\n    expect((await resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'general\', mode: \'learn\', difficulty: \'medium\' })).route).toContain(\'phase=conditional\');\\r\\n    expect((await resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'area\', area: \'quantifiers\', mode: \'practice\', difficulty: \'beginner\' })).route).toContain(\'area=quantifiers\');\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'does not consider different skill, mode or level assignments duplicates\', () => {\\r\\n    const target = { scope: \'skill\', unitId: \'group-1\', mode: \'practice\', difficulty: \'medium\' } as const;\\r\\n    expect(new Set([conditionalTargetKey(), conditionalTargetKey(target), conditionalTargetKey({ ...target, unitId: \'group-2\' }), conditionalTargetKey({ ...target, difficulty: \'advanced\' }), conditionalTargetKey({ ...target, mode: \'learn\' })]).size).toBe(5);\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'rejects unknown skills and areas before assigning any work\', async () => {\\r\\n    await expect(resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'skill\', mode: \'practice\', unitId: \'missing\', difficulty: \'beginner\' })).rejects.toThrow(\'existing conditional skill\');\\r\\n    await expect(resolveConditionalAssignment({ scope: \'area\', mode: \'practice\', area: \'missing\' as never, difficulty: \'beginner\' })).rejects.toThrow(\'existing conditional area\');\\r\\n  });\\r\\n  it(\'resolves the registry and gate for targeted assignment links\', () => {\\r\\n    expect(findBootcampByRoute(\'/bootcamp/conditionals/practice?unit=group-1&level=medium\')?.id).toBe(\'conditionals\');\\r\\n    expect(classroomItemMatchesBootcamp({ source_id: text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nimport re,json\nfrom pathlib import Path\np=Path('src/bootcamps/conditionals/content/foundations.source.ts')\nwith p.open(encoding='utf-8',newline='') as f:s=f.read()\nfirst={\n'orientation-guide': \"Card L's number is registered: L is activated, so the rule covers L.\",\n'orientation-b1': '“If the desk is staffed, the bell is answered” gives an exceptionless rule: staffing guarantees an answer to the bell.',\n'orientation-b2': '“Crate M is numbered” lets us apply the rule to M and conclude that M has a label.',\n'orientation-b3': '“Book 4 has a new binding” reports a fact about that particular book.',\n'orientation-m1': \"Quiet operation isn't guaranteed. An inspected lift could be noisy without contradicting a claim about what usually happens.\",\n'orientation-m2': \"The roof's panel status is unknown. A support frame is required for a panel, but the rule hasn't said every framed roof has one.\",\n'orientation-m3': \"Every sealed sample is logged, but the rule doesn't establish that any sealed or logged sample exists.\",\n'orientation-a1': 'Imani may request a copy because Imani is one of the authorized visitors the rule covers.',\n'orientation-a2': \"Neither housing conclusion is established. Indoor certification doesn't meet the rule's condition of certification for underwater use.\",\n'orientation-a3': 'The instruction calls for shutdown because the warning light is flashing.',\n'sufficient-necessary-guide': 'A verified account without a recovery code breaks the rule: every verified account has to have that code.',\n'sufficient-necessary-b1': 'Being fire-rated is enough to establish a steel shell for this cabinet. That makes fire-rated status the sufficient condition here.',\n'sufficient-necessary-b2': 'Having an assessment is necessary for accreditation. An accredited course without one would break the rule.',\n'sufficient-necessary-b3': \"A tube that isn't packaged and has a cap fits the rule. The sentence promises caps for packaged tubes without restricting caps to them.\",\n'sufficient-necessary-m1': 'Reflective is necessary in the first rule and sufficient in the second. Polishing requires reflection; reflection is enough to establish visibility.',\n'sufficient-necessary-m2': \"F's tagged status is unknown. Tagged specimens must be stored, but the sentence doesn't restrict storage to tagged specimens.\",\n'sufficient-necessary-m3': \"The case is allowed: an unupgraded seat without extra legroom doesn't break a promise about upgraded seats.\",\n'sufficient-necessary-a1': 'Acceptance for the student symposium is sufficient for sponsorship. The rule promises a faculty sponsor for a paper with that exact acceptance.',\n'sufficient-necessary-a2': \"Validity without authentication fits the rule. The promise covers authenticated tokens; it hasn't said every valid token is authenticated.\",\n'sufficient-necessary-a3': 'Routing through Server C is necessary for priority status, but no priority packet is established by the rule alone.',\n'group-1-guide': 'Rover crosses ridge → report appears keeps the stated direction: crossing the ridge is enough for a report to appear.',\n'group-1-b1': 'Reserved locker → displays nameplate matches the rule: a reserved locker has to display a nameplate.',\n'group-1-b2': 'The woven basket has a reinforced rim because it is one of the baskets the rule covers.',\n'group-1-b3': 'The schedule changing is what guarantees printing. Whenever the schedule changes, the rule promises a printed notice.',\n'group-1-b4': 'Shelf has a blue label goes left. The rule makes that complete condition enough for its books to be available to borrow.',\n'group-1-b5': 'Cup packed in gift set → has matching saucer keeps both the direction and the restriction to cups in that gift set.',\n'group-1-b6': 'The dessert is chilled. The rule says every dessert on the lunch menu has to be a chilled dessert.',\n'group-1-b7': 'Ticket bought online is the complete sufficient idea. The promise covers tickets with that purchase method.',\n'group-1-m1': 'Invoice submitted through portal → time-stamped preserves the full condition: the invoice was submitted through the portal.',\n'group-1-m2': 'Recorded rehearsal → held in Studio B matches the sentence: a recorded rehearsal has to be one held there.',\n'group-1-m3': \"Ice detection is unknown. Detecting ice guarantees a raised flag, but a raised flag hasn't been made enough to establish ice detection.\",\n'group-1-m4': 'Sealed lid → green sticker matches the sentence. The sealed lid is enough for the sticker even though the if-clause comes second.',\n'group-1-m5': \"The rehearsal's ending is not established. Its ending guarantees dim lights, but dim lights don't establish its ending.\",\n'group-1-m6': \"A lower-shelf mug with a handle fits. The rule promises handles for top-shelf mugs without restricting handles to that shelf.\",\n'group-1-m7': 'Guest → Seat number loses the before-six restriction. The sentence covers guests arriving before six.',\n'group-1-a1': \"Captioning is not settled. Selection for an indoor series doesn't meet the rule's condition of selection for the outdoor series.\",\n'group-1-a2': 'Neither arrow follows: “asked if” reports an inquiry about insurance rather than a conditional guarantee.',\n'group-1-a3-v2': 'Certified for heat exposure → stored in outdoor cabinet preserves both the certification limit and the specified storage place.',\n'group-1-a4': 'No conditional rule is established. The sentence reports one visit when the shutters were closed.',\n'group-1-a5': \"The lunch case doesn't contradict the rule. Lunch reservation alone doesn't meet the complete evening-service condition.\",\n'group-1-a6': 'Notebook sold in travel kit → waterproof cover is the same rule: a notebook sold in that kit has to have a waterproof cover.',\n'group-1-a7': \"The workshop fits the rule. Online format is required for same-day feedback, but online format hasn't been made enough for that feedback.\",\n'group-1-a8': 'Order includes collection time → confirmation appears keeps the complete sufficient idea, even though it comes second in the sentence.',\n'group-1-a9': \"The rule doesn't establish Q's reservation. A nut-free reservation guarantees a green marker, but the marker doesn't establish that reservation.\",\n'group-2-guide': 'Can access recording → subscriber preserves the restriction: someone with access permission has to be a subscriber.',\n'group-2-b1': 'Display activates → battery charged preserves the requirement. If the display activates, its battery has to be charged.',\n'group-2-b2': 'Being a resident is necessary for receiving a parking sticker: the rule restricts recipients to residents.',\n'group-2-b3': 'Registered buoy → broadcasts identifier follows: every registered buoy broadcasts its identifier.',\n'group-2-b4': 'Kitchen is open goes right because an open kitchen is required for the café to serve breakfast.',\n'group-2-b5': 'Delivery service runs → road access keeps the location requirement: wherever the service runs, road access has to be available there.',\n'group-2-b6': 'Having a signed form is necessary for borrowing a camera. The rule says the borrower needs that complete requirement.',\n'group-2-b7': 'The captain is required to attend the briefing. “Must” gives an obligation rather than a report of attendance.',\n'group-2-m1': \"Mira's entry is not established. A qualifying score is required for entry, but the rule hasn't made that score enough.\",\n'group-2-m2-v2': 'Camera records → motion detected preserves the requirement: if the camera records, motion is detected.',\n'group-2-m3': 'Satellite contact is available at P. The beacon operates there, and operation requires contact at that same location.',\n'group-2-m4': \"Order paid in advance is necessary for being shipped today. The sentence restricts today's shipments to those orders.\",\n'group-2-m5': \"Locker allocation remains unknown. A day pass is required for a locker, but the rule doesn't promise a locker to every passholder.\",\n'group-2-m6': 'Print from this edition → date stamp preserves the scope: the promise applies to prints from this edition.',\n'group-2-m7': \"Dana's key return doesn't prove renter status. Renters have an obligation to return the key, but the rule doesn't restrict key returns to renters.\",\n'group-2-a1-v2': 'May inspect these records → reviewer appointed for emergency panel preserves permission on the left and the complete reviewer appointment on the right.',\n'group-2-a2': 'Rowan is required to submit a signed safety declaration because Rowan holds a night-filming permit covered by the policy.',\n'group-2-a3-v2': 'Eligible claim → filed by deadline through official portal preserves both limits of the requirement.',\n'group-2-a4': 'Replacement sent → damaged item returned with receipt keeps the complete requirement for a replacement.',\n'group-2-a5': \"The facts don't establish whether the requirement is met. A screening needs a shaded seating area, and seats alone don't establish shade.\",\n'group-2-a6': \"The item's queue is not established. Express-queue items receive a collection number, but the rule doesn't restrict those numbers to that queue.\",\n'group-2-a7': \"This camera used underwater → has waterproof case preserves the requirement for underwater use, even though that requirement appears first in the sentence.\",\n}\ndec=json.JSONDecoder();seen=[];oldcontracts=[];newcontracts=[]\nlines=s.splitlines(keepends=True)\nfor i,line in enumerate(lines):\n if not re.search(r'question\\(\"(?:orientation|sufficient-necessary|group-[12])-',line):continue\n start=line.index('question(')+len('question(');rest=line[start:];args=[];positions=[];offset=start\n for k in range(6):\n  value,n=dec.raw_decode(rest);args.append(value);positions.append((offset,offset+n))\n  remaining=rest[n:];stripped=remaining.lstrip(', ');offset+=n+len(remaining)-len(stripped);rest=stripped\n qid=args[0];seen.append(qid);oldcontracts.append(args[:4])\n steps=args[5];steps[0]=first[qid];a,b=positions[5]\n line=line[:a]+json.dumps(steps,ensure_ascii=False)+line[b:]\n if qid=='orientation-m1':\n  line=line.replace('Most inspected lifts operate quietly.', 'Inspected lifts usually operate quietly.')\n  line=line.replace(\"without contradicting most?\", \"without contradicting usually?\")\n # Verify immutable answer coordinates directly from the resulting source.\n rest=line[start:];out=[]\n for k in range(4):\n  value,n=dec.raw_decode(rest);out.append(value);rest=rest[n:].lstrip(', ')\n newcontracts.append(out);lines[i]=line\nassert len(seen)==66 and set(seen)==set(first),(len(seen),set(first)-set(seen))\nfor old,new in zip(oldcontracts,newcontracts):\n assert old[0]==new[0] and old[2:]==new[2:],old[0]\n assert old[1]==new[1] or old[0]=='orientation-m1',old[0]\ns=''.join(lines)\ns=s.replace('title: \"Enough to Guarantee, Required to Follow\"','title: \"What is enough? What is required?\"')\ns=s.replace('title: \"Group 1: Identify What Guarantees What\"','title: \"Group 1: what is enough?\"')\ns=s.replace('title: \"Group 2: Keep the Requirement on the Right\"','title: \"Group 2: what is required?\"')\ns=s.replace('Ask what the expression refers to, then read your arrow in English. Keep the full before-noon submission condition on the sufficient side.')\nwith p.open('w',encoding='utf-8',newline='') as f:f.write(s)\nprint('Updated 62 practice and four guided first-step explanations; all IDs, options, keys and counts unchanged.
