Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s browser corrections, 2026-10-03: define “guarantee” as the certainty supplied by the stated rule before using it to explain another idea. Explain “direction” through an actual relationship before using the word as advice. A beginner needs to see what the arrow means, which complete idea belongs on each side, and why sentence order need not match diagram order. “Read left to right” alone does not teach that. Introduce sufficient as enough and necessary as required through the example, then develop their limits.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor rule versus fact, make the hypothetical explicit: a rule tells us what would follow **if** a condition were true. It does not report that the condition happened. Contrast “Suppose this order is marked ready” with “This order is marked ready” before drawing a conclusion about an actual case. Keep the anchor sentence idiomatic and complete. Emphasis and motion around “if” should reveal that hypothetical relationship, with equally clear static information when motion is reduced.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntroduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nA repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition\'s shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.\\r\\n\\r\\nTransfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader\'s desire for certainty a recognizable shape.\\r\\n\\r\\nHumor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua\'s source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source\'s distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.\\r\\n\\r\\nNever turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua\'s biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision\\r\\n\\r\\nBold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.\\r\\n- Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.\\r\\n- Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.\\r\\n- Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.\\r\\n- Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.\\r\\n- Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.\\r\\n- Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Their local draft status is not proof of posting.\\r\\n * Recall and Rephrase instructions are an authored adaptation of archived homework\\r\\n * naming that exercise; the archive does not supply its complete original procedure.\\r\\n * Extended expression families also reference Asia\'s assigned 2026-08-24\\r\\n * Conditional Logic Cheat Sheet and The Breakdown; contextual meaning takes\\r\\n * precedence over their mechanical word-list shortcuts.\\r\\n * All scenarios, questions and explanations below are newly authored, not official LSAT items.\\r\\n * Difficulty concerns interpretation and inference. These sets prescribe no mastery threshold or clock.\\r\\n */\\r\\nconst question = (\\r\\n  id: string, prompt: string, options: string[], answerIndex: number,\\r\\n  hint: string, reasoningSteps: string[],\\r\\n): ConditionalQuestion => ({ id, prompt, options, answerIndex, hint, reasoningSteps, explanation: reasoningSteps.join(\' \') });\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const foundationUnits: ConditionalUnit[] = [\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    id: \'orientation\', title: \\"What Are Conditionals?\\", area: \'understanding\', phase: \'conditional\', prerequisites: [],\\r\\n    goal: \\"Explain what a conditional says, use it with a fact, and say what the information leaves open.\\",\\r\\n    teaching: [\\r\\n      \\"A conditional tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. A condition is something we can say about a situation, such as an order being marked ready or its having been packed. The conditional connects those two ideas. It doesn\'t tell us that either one has happened.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Why learn this? A conclusion on the LSAT can depend on which way that connection runs. If you know what the rule promises, you can explain why a conclusion follows. If an answer uses the rule in the other direction, you can see the missing connection instead of accepting a conclusion that sounds reasonable.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Let\'s use one rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. Take that claim as true for this exercise. Every ready order has to be packed. Orders marked ready are usually packed would say something weaker, because usually leaves room for an exception. Our rule doesn\'t.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Now we get a fact: Order A is marked ready. What do you know about A? Take a moment to explain your answer before reading on. A has been packed. The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact puts A in that group. A ready order that hadn\'t been packed would break the rule.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Keep the rule and change the fact: Order B has been packed. Must B be marked ready? Try to explain why before reading on. We don\'t know. The rule told us about ready orders. It didn\'t say every packed order is marked ready. B could be marked ready or packed without being marked ready. Either possibility fits what we\'ve been told.\\",\\r\\n      \\"We can now shorten the relationship to Marked ready → Packed. Read that as: if an order is marked ready, it has been packed. The arrow records the connection we\'ve already understood. It doesn\'t add a cause, a time sequence, or a fact about a particular order.\\",\\r\\n      \\"In the practice, separate the rule from the fact about the case. Then ask what that fact lets you conclude. Write the reason as well as the answer. That gives you something specific to compare with the explanation if your choice goes wrong.\\",\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    method: [\\r\\n      \\"Read the rule in ordinary English: what does it say has to be true if the first condition is true?\\",\\r\\n      \\"Check the strength. Every ready order is packed gives a guarantee; usually packed allows an exception.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Find the separate fact about the case. A rule alone doesn\'t identify a ready order.\\",\\r\\n      \\"Compare the whole fact with the condition the rule covers. Order A is marked ready meets our ready-order condition.\\",\\r\\n      \\"State what follows and why. If the information leaves a question open, show how both possibilities could fit.\\",\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    workedExample: {\\r\\n      statements: [\\"Every order marked ready has been packed.\\", \\"Order A is marked ready.\\"],\\r\\n      reasoning: [\\"The rule tells us that every ready order has been packed. The fact identifies A as one of those orders.\\", \\"So A has been packed. We haven\'t assumed anything about a different order or about what made A ready.\\"],\\r\\n      conclusion: \\"Order A has been packed.\\",\\r\\n    },\\r\\n    guidedExample: question(\\"orientation-guide\\", \\"If a card is activated, its number is registered. Card L is activated. What follows?\\", [\\"Card L\'s number is registered.\\", \\"Every registered number belongs to an activated card.\\", \\"No other card is registered.\\"], 0, \\"Card L is activated. Which part of the rule does that fact meet?\\", [\\"Card L\'s number is registered: L is activated, so the rule covers L.\\", \\"So L\'s number is registered. It doesn\'t give a reverse rule about every registered number or exclude other registered cards.\\"]),\\r\\n    commonMistake: { mistake: \\"Treating an imagined case as something we\'ve actually been told.\\", repair: \\"If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed. That rule lets us imagine an order with the ready label and know what would follow. It doesn\'t say an order actually has the label. Suppose Order A is marked ready is a situation to consider. Order A is marked ready is a fact the question gives us. With that fact, we can conclude A has been packed. Without it, we haven\'t established anything about A.\\" },\\r\\n    drills: {\\r\\n      beginner: [\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-b1\\", \\"Which statement gives an exceptionless rule?\\", [\\"The desk is staffed now.\\", \\"If the desk is staffed, the bell is answered.\\", \\"The bell is usually answered.\\"], 1, \\"Which sentence promises one condition whenever the other is true?\\", [\\"“If the desk is staffed, the bell is answered” gives an exceptionless rule: staffing guarantees an answer to the bell.\\", \\"Staffed now reports one fact. Usually answered allows an exception. Neither gives that same connection.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-b2\\", \\"Every numbered crate has a label. Which additional fact lets you conclude that Crate M has a label?\\", [\\"Crate M is numbered.\\", \\"Crate M is heavy.\\", \\"Crate M might be numbered.\\"], 0, \\"What kind of crate does the rule promise a label for? Find a fact putting M in that group.\\", [\\"“Crate M is numbered” lets us apply the rule to M and conclude that M has a label.\\", \\"Crate M is numbered puts M in that group. Heavy doesn\'t do that, and might be numbered leaves it unsettled.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-b3\\", \\"Which claim reports a case fact?\\", [\\"All restored books have new bindings.\\", \\"Book 4 has a new binding.\\", \\"Restored books generally look newer.\\"], 1, \\"Look for a claim about one named book rather than a rule or tendency.\\", [\\"“Book 4 has a new binding” reports a fact about that particular book.\\", \\"All restored books gives a general rule. Generally look newer describes a tendency. Neither reports this single binding fact.\\"]),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      medium: [\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-m1\\", \\"Inspected lifts usually operate quietly. Lift 2 was inspected. Is quiet operation guaranteed?\\", [\\"Yes.\\", \\"No; quiet operation is impossible.\\", \\"No; it remains unsettled.\\"], 2, \\"Could Lift 2 be an inspected lift that doesn\'t operate quietly without contradicting usually?\\", [\\"Quiet operation isn\'t guaranteed. An inspected lift could be noisy without contradicting a claim about what usually happens.\\", \\"We know Lift 2 was inspected, but we don\'t know which kind it is. Quiet operation is possible, not guaranteed.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-m2\\", \\"If a roof has a solar panel, it has a support frame. A roof has a support frame. What is established?\\", [\\"It has a solar panel.\\", \\"It has no solar panel.\\", \\"Its panel status is unknown.\\"], 2, \\"The fact gives you the support frame. What did the rule actually promise about framed roofs?\\", [\\"The roof\'s panel status is unknown. A support frame is required for a panel, but the rule hasn\'t said every framed roof has one.\\", \\"This roof could have a panel or have a frame for another reason. Neither panel status follows from the information.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-m3\\", \\"Every sealed sample is logged. No sample is said to be sealed. Which conclusion follows?\\", [\\"A logged sample exists.\\", \\"Every sealed sample is logged, but existence remains unknown.\\", \\"No samples are logged.\\"], 1, \\"Does every sealed sample is logged tell you that a sealed sample exists?\\", [\\"Every sealed sample is logged, but the rule doesn\'t establish that any sealed or logged sample exists.\\", \\"It hasn\'t identified a sealed sample or any other logged sample. The relationship remains true while existence remains unknown.\\"]),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      advanced: [\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-a1\\", \\"Every visitor authorized to enter the archive may request a copy. Imani is a visitor authorized to enter the archive. What is guaranteed?\\", [\\"Imani requests a copy.\\", \\"Imani may request a copy.\\", \\"Imani receives a copy.\\"], 1, \\"Keep may request in your conclusion. Is permission the same as making or receiving a request?\\", [\\"Imani may request a copy because Imani is one of the authorized visitors the rule covers.\\", \\"It doesn\'t say Imani uses that permission or receives a copy. May request is the conclusion the wording supports.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-a2\\", \\"Every sensor certified for underwater use has a sealed housing. Sensor K is certified for indoor use. What follows about its housing?\\", [\\"It is sealed.\\", \\"It is not sealed.\\", \\"Neither conclusion is established.\\"], 2, \\"Does certified for indoor use meet certified for underwater use?\\", [\\"Neither housing conclusion is established. Indoor certification doesn\'t meet the rule\'s condition of certification for underwater use.\\", \\"Indoor certification doesn\'t establish that condition. K could have a sealed housing or lack one without contradicting what we\'ve been told.\\"]),\\r\\n        question(\\"orientation-a3\\", \\"The manual says: If the warning light flashes, shut down the machine. The light flashes. What follows from this instruction alone?\\", [\\"The machine has shut down.\\", \\"The instruction calls for shutdown.\\", \\"The machine cannot shut down.\\"], 1, \\"The light flashes. What response does the instruction require, and what action has actually been reported?\\", [\\"The instruction calls for shutdown because the warning light is flashing.\\", \\"The instruction therefore calls for shutdown. It doesn\'t report that anyone followed it or that the machine has already shut down.\\"]),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n    },\\r\\n  },\\r\\n"}}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"00eb0b","wall_time_seconds":2.5163164,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6490,"output":"671:       prompt: \'A complaint has been filed. Does that alone establish that a review occurs?\',\\r\\n672:       reasoning: [\\r\\n673:         \\"No. A filed complaint with no review fits the sentence. The statement restricts reviews to occasions with a filed complaint; it doesn\'t say every complaint produces one.\\",\\r\\n674:         \\"Review occurs → Complaint filed keeps the required occasion on the right. If we learn that a review occurs, this rule lets us conclude a complaint was filed.\\",\\r\\n675:       ],\\r\\n676:     },\\r\\n677:     {\\r\\n678:       title: \'Only where: keep the requirement at the same place\',\\r\\n679:       paragraphs: [\\r\\n680:         \\"The service needs a station wherever it operates. Only where points to station availability as the requirement.\\",\\r\\n681:         \\"Compare the Group 1 sentence: Where a station is available, the service operates. That made availability enough. This sentence requires availability for operation. Adding only changes the direction even though both sentences mention the same ideas.\\",\\r\\n682:       ],\\r\\n683:       prompt: \'The service operates at South Square. What must be true there?\',\\r\\n684:       reasoning: [\\r\\n685:         \\"A station is available there. Operation at South Square meets the condition the rule tells us about, so availability is required at that same location.\\",\\r\\n686:         \\"A station somewhere else wouldn\'t satisfy this requirement. Read the diagram as: if the service operates at a location, a station is available there.\\",\\r\\n687:       ],\\r\\n688:     },\\r\\n689:     {\\r\\n690:       title: \'Always: identify what holds in every covered case\',\\r\\n691:       paragraphs: [\\r\\n692:         \\"Read the subject before deciding what always does. This sentence tells us that receiving an invitation holds in every case of someone being a finalist.\\",\\r\\n693:         \\"Every finalist receives an invitation, so receiving one is necessary for finalist status. Always modifies receives an invitation. A familiar word helps only after we understand its job in the whole sentence.\\",\\r\\n694:       ],\\r\\n695:       prompt: \'What is guaranteed here: an invitation for a finalist, or finalist status for anyone invited?\',\\r\\n696:       reasoning: [\\r\\n697:         \\"An invitation for a finalist. The sentence gives one to every person who meets that condition.\\",\\r\\n698:         \\"Always hasn\'t told us that invitations go only to finalists. The invitation belongs on the right as the result each finalist must receive; starting from it would reverse the relationship.\\",\\r\\n699:       ],\\r\\n700:     },\\r\\n701:     {\\r\\n702:       title: \'Requires: the target can come before the word\',\\r\\n703:       paragraphs: [\\r\\n704:         \\"What does entry require? A current pass. That complete idea is necessary. A pass that has expired doesn\'t meet the condition the sentence gives us.\\",\\r\\n705:         \\"Now move the requirement: A current pass is required for entry. The pass comes before required, but its role hasn\'t changed. Automatically taking the words after the indicator would point you toward entry and reverse the rule.\\",\\r\\n706:       ],\\r\\n707:       prompt: \'How would you diagram: A current pass is required for entry?\',\\r\\n708:       reasoning: [\\r\\n709:         \\"Enters → Has current pass. If someone enters, the required pass is present. The target is a current pass, wherever that phrase appears in the sentence.\\",\\r\\n710:         \\"Having the pass still doesn\'t guarantee entry. Someone could keep one in a drawer and never enter. Required means needed for the other condition, not enough to establish that it happened.\\",\\r\\n711:       ],\\r\\n712:     },\\r\\n713:     {\\r\\n714:       title: \'Must: preserve an obligation as an obligation\',\\r\\n715:       paragraphs: [\\r\\n716:         \\"Here must states what finalists are required to do. If someone is a finalist, that person has an obligation to attend the interview.\\",\\r\\n717:         \\"The sentence hasn\'t supplied a fact proving that every finalist complied. Keep required to in the complete target so that your diagram preserves the difference between an obligation and a completed action.\\",\\r\\n718:       ],\\r\\n719:       prompt: \'Maya is a finalist. Which follows: she is required to attend, or she actually attended?\',\\r\\n720:       reasoning: [\\r\\n721:         \\"She is required to attend. Maya is a finalist, and the sentence gives every finalist that obligation.\\",\\r\\n722:         \\"Actual attendance would need information about what Maya did. Finalist → Attends interview would quietly turn the stated requirement into proof of compliance.\\",\\r\\n723:       ],\\r\\n724:       after: [\\r\\n725:         \\"Shortening a label should save writing while preserving the claim. Required to attend interview is longer than attends interview, but here those extra words are doing necessary work.\\",\\r\\n726:       ],\\r\\n727:     },\\r\\n728:   ],\\r\\n729: };\\r\\n730: \\r\\n731: function IndicatorSections({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n732:   return <>{indicatorExplanations[id].map((lesson, index) => {\\r\\n733:     const item = translationExamples[id][index];\\r\\n734:     return <Section key={item.word} title={lesson.title} expressionNumber={index + 1}>\\r\\n735:       <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>\\r\\n736:       {lesson.paragraphs.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\\r\\n737:       {id === \'group-2\' && item.word === \'only if\' && <>\\r\\n738:         <p>Try reading the sentence as though it concerns you. Imagine that you graduate and ask what you now have to know about your project. Then try starting from project completion and see whether the sentence settles graduation.</p>\\r\\n739:         <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Put yourself in the proposed starting condition, then check what the original sentence requires you to know. We\'re checking its meaning, not predicting what usually happens to graduates.</p>\\r\\n740:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I graduate, what must be true of my project?\\">\\r\\n741:           <p>I\'ve completed it. The sentence requires project completion for anyone who graduates, including me in this imagined case.</p>\\r\\n742:           <p>Graduates → Completes project matches that reading. Now try the reverse below.</p>\\r\\n743:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n744:       </>}\\r\\n745:       <ThinkPause prompt={lesson.prompt}>\\r\\n746:         {lesson.reasoning.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\\r\\n747:         <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>\\r\\n748:         <p className=\\"co-readback\\">{item.readback}</p>\\r\\n749:       </ThinkPause>\\r\\n750:       {lesson.after?.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}\\r\\n751:       {lesson.check && <Check {...lesson.check}/>}\\r\\n752:       {id === \'group-2\' && item.word === \'only if\' && <DirectionSwitch/>}\\r\\n753:     </Section>;\\r\\n754:   })}</>;\\r\\n755: }\\r\\n756: \\r\\n757: function Fundamentals({ roles = false }: { roles?: boolean }) {\\r\\n758:   return <>\\r\\n759:     {!roles && <>\\r\\n760:       <Section title=\\"One condition tells you something else\\">\\r\\n761:         <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something we\'re considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. We can ask whether each one is true.</p>\\r\\n762:         <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn\'t tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow <strong className=\\"co-if-inline\\">if</strong> it did.</p>\\r\\n763:         <p>Think of that if as an invitation to imagine a situation. Suppose the first condition is true. What would you then know? We can ask that question without claiming the situation really happened.</p>\\r\\n764:         <IfConnection/>\\r\\n765:         <p>You don\'t need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>\\r\\n766:       </Section>\\r\\n767:       <Section title=\\"Why learn this?\\" dark>\\r\\n768:         <p>An LSAT question gives you some information and asks what you can conclude from it. Sometimes that information is a rule about what has to be true if something else is true. You need to know what the rule says before using it.</p>\\r\\n769:         <p>A rule can tell you something about an order, a person, or a decision. It can also leave questions unanswered. We\'ll practice telling the difference between what we\'ve been told and what we\'re adding ourselves.</p>\\r\\n770:         <p>Let\'s begin with one rule. We\'ll read it, imagine a case that meets its condition, and ask what we know about that same case.</p>\\r\\n771:       </Section>\\r\\n772:       <Section title=\\"Read one rule in ordinary English\\">\\r\\n773:         <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n774:         <p>There are two ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready for pickup</strong>, and it <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence connects them. Imagine an order with that ready label. The rule tells you it has been packed.</p>\\r\\n775:         <p>We haven\'t been told that any particular order has that label. We\'re saying what would be true <strong>if</strong> it did. That\'s the difference between a rule and a fact about an actual order.</p>\\r\\n776:       </Section>\\r\\n777:       <Section title=\\"Enough on one side, required on the other\\">\\r\\n778:         <p>Suppose an order is marked ready for pickup. Is that enough to know it\'s packed? Yes. The sentence says an order with that label has been packed. We don\'t need a second fact about its contents.</p>\\r\\n779:         <p>That\'s a <strong>guarantee</strong>: the rule doesn\'t leave room for a ready order that hasn\'t been packed. It gives us something certain about any order that meets that condition.</p>\\r\\n780:         <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient means enough</h3><p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know the order has been packed. We call it the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>.</p></div><div><h3>Necessary means required</h3><p>An order with that ready label has to have been packed. Packing is required for it. We call it the <strong>necessary condition</strong>.</p></div></div>\\r\\n781:         <p>These are two jobs in one rule. One condition is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first is true. We\'ll give each job more attention in the next lesson.</p>\\r\\n782:       </Section>\\r\\n783:       <Section title=\\"A guarantee is stronger than a tendency\\">\\r\\n784:         <p>Now compare a guarantee with something that is only usually true.</p>\\r\\n785:         <div className=\\"co-intro-relationship\\"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready for pickup are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</strong><p>Every order with that label has to be packed.</p></div></div>\\r\\n786:         <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. A ready order that hasn\'t been packed could fit the first statement. It couldn\'t fit the second.</p>\\r\\n787:         <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we\'re asking what follows from this statement. We aren\'t checking whether the shop follows its rule in real life.</p>\\r\\n788:       </Section>\\r\\n789:       <Section title=\\"Use a fact about one order\\">\\r\\n790:         <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n791:         <p>Now add a fact: <strong>Order A is marked ready for pickup.</strong> This is no longer just an imagined case. The question has told us that A meets the condition.</p>\\r\\n792:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order A is marked ready for pickup. What do we now know about it?\\">\\r\\n793:           <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order with that ready label. The fact tells us A has it. Together, they let us conclude A has been packed.</p>\\r\\n794:           <p>The rule alone didn\'t tell us A was ready. The separate fact did that. Our conclusion is about A, not every order in the shop.</p>\\r\\n795:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n796:       </Section>\\r\\n797:       <Section title=\\"The arrow is a shorter way to write it\\">\\r\\n798:         <p>We can record that relationship with shorter labels. Here, <strong>Marked ready</strong> means marked ready for pickup, and <strong>Packed</strong> means the same order has been packed.</p>\\r\\n799:         <Arrow left=\\"Marked ready\\" right=\\"Packed\\"/>\\r\\n800:         <p>The sufficient condition goes on the left: it gives us enough to know the other condition is true. The necessary condition goes on the right: it\'s required whenever the left condition is true.</p>\\r\\n801:         <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>: if the order is marked ready for pickup, it must have been packed. It doesn\'t mean “and then,” and it doesn\'t say the label causes packing. This is a connection between what is true, not a timeline.</p>\\r\\n802:         <p>English doesn\'t always put the sufficient idea first. <strong>An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup</strong> expresses the same rule with the ideas in a different order. The ready label still gives us packing, so the diagram stays the same. Meaning decides the two sides.</p>\\r\\n803:       </Section>\\r\\n804:       <Section title=\\"Change the fact, keep the rule\\">\\r\\n805:         <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, we\'re told about packing instead of the ready label.</p>\\r\\n806:         <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n807:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready for pickup?\\">\\r\\n808:           <p><strong>We don\'t know.</strong> B could have the ready label. It could also be packed without that label. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>\\r\\n809:           <p>The rule tells us about orders marked ready for pickup. It hasn\'t said every packed order gets that label. Packing alone doesn\'t answer whether B is ready.</p>\\r\\n810:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n811:         <p>In the drawing, orders marked ready sit inside packed orders. Every ready order has to be packed. A packed order could be inside or outside the ready group. Compare the two facts to see what each one tells us.</p>\\r\\n812:         <SetExplorer/>\\r\\n813:       </Section>\\r\\n814:       <Section title=\\"Try the same reasoning with a new rule\\">\\r\\n815:         <p>You used the ready label to establish packing. Starting with packing left the label unknown. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>\\r\\n816:         <Check prompt=\\"Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?\\" options={[\'Yes, the badge proves it.\', \'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.\', \'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.\']}/>\\r\\n817:         <p>A badge may make volunteer status seem plausible. Check whether the sentence actually told you that everyone with a badge is a volunteer. This one didn\'t.</p>\\r\\n818:       </Section>\\r\\n819:       <Section title=\\"Keep what the sentence actually gives you\\">\\r\\n820:         <p>Keep the sentence\'s own strength. A rule can give you permission to do something without saying you did it. It can also tell you what you\'re supposed to do without reporting that you followed the instruction. Those are different claims.</p>\\r\\n821:         <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote>\\r\\n822:         <p>May use gives permission here. A guest with a ticket is allowed to use the cloakroom. That guest could leave their coat on and never go inside. The permission is still there.</p>\\r\\n823:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Lena has a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\">\\r\\n824:           <p>No. We know she\'s allowed to use it because the rule gives that permission to every guest with a ticket. We haven\'t been told whether she used it.</p>\\r\\n825:           <p>Compare this instruction: <strong>If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack.</strong> A wet coat makes the instruction call for hanging it up. That doesn\'t report anyone carrying out the instruction. We\'d need a fact about what they actually did.</p>\\r\\n826:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n827:         <Check prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\\r\\n828:           \\"You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice tells us what to do with an empty tray; it hasn\'t reported that anyone did it.\\",\\r\\n829:           \\"The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone has returned it is a separate question.\\",\\r\\n830:           \\"The notice gives an instruction to return the tray. It doesn\'t say returning it is impossible. Keep what the sentence directs separate from what has actually happened.\\",\\r\\n831:         ]}/>\\r\\n832:         <p>Before drawing a conclusion, ask what the rule gives you: a fact, permission, or an instruction. Keep that meaning in your answer. Allowed to use isn\'t used, and told to return isn\'t returned.</p>\\r\\n833:       </Section>\\r\\n834:     </>}\\r\\n835:     {roles && <>\\r\\n836:       <Section title=\\"Sufficient means enough\\">\\r\\n837:         <p>Being marked ready for pickup is enough to know an order has been packed. That makes the ready label <strong>sufficient</strong> for packing. Sufficient means enough.</p>\\r\\n838:         <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n839:         <p>The rule already tells you what has to be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>\\r\\n840:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?\\">\\r\\n841:           <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>\\r\\n842:           <p>This doesn\'t make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>\\r\\n843:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n844:       </Section>\\r\\n845:       <Section title=\\"Enough does not mean the only way\\">\\r\\n846:         <p>Suppose Order D isn\'t marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn\'t settle the packing question.</p>\\r\\n847:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Order D isn\'t marked ready. Could it still have been packed?\\">\\r\\n848:           <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn\'t been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>\\r\\n849:           <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn\'t said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn\'t present, the other condition may still be true.</p>\\r\\n850:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n851:         <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>\\r\\n852:         <Check prompt=\\"Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?\\" options={[\'Marked collected\', \'Has a pickup receipt\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.\', \'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.\']}/>\\r\\n853:       </Section>\\r\\n854:       <Section title=\\"Necessary means required\\">\\r\\n855:         <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready for pickup has to have been packed. Packing is required for an order with that label. That makes being packed <strong>necessary</strong> for being marked ready.</p>\\r\\n856:         <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>\\r\\n857:         <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>\\r\\n858:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Which condition does a ready order have to meet?\\">\\r\\n859:           <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we\'d have a ready order that wasn\'t packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>\\r\\n860:           <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n861:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n862:         <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n863:         <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>\\r\\n864:         <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n865:       </Section>\\r\\n866:       <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n867:         <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n868:         <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n869:         <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n870:         <Arrow left=\\"Enters the archive\\" right=\\"Has a pass\\"/>\\r\\n871:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?\\">\\r\\n872:           <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\r\\n873:           <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n874:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n875:         <Check prompt=\\"Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?\\" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', \\"You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too.\\"]}/>\\r\\n876:       </Section>\\r\\n877:       <Section title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n878:         <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n879:         <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n880:         <blockquote>If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.</blockquote>\\r\\n881:         <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>\\r\\n882:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?\\">\\r\\n883:           <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\\r\\n884:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n885:         <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?\\">\\r\\n886:           <p>No. My order could be packed without a ready label. That fits every ready order is packed because it doesn\'t create a ready order that hasn\'t been packed.</p>\\r\\n887:           <p>Packed → Marked ready would add a claim about every packed order. The sentence doesn\'t give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>\\r\\n888:         </ThinkPause>\\r\\n889:       </Section>\\r\\n890:       <Section title=\\"Read the roles in the relationship\\">\\r\\n891:         <p>In <strong>Marked ready → Packed</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it\'s enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it\'s required whenever the left holds.</p>\\r\\n892:         <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don\'t ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>\\r\\n893:         <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\r\\n894:       </Section>\\r\\n895:     </>}\\r\\n896:   </>;\\r\\n897: }\\r\\n898: \\r\\n899: function GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n900:   const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === \'group-1\' ? \'g1\' : \'g2\'))!;\\r\\n901:   const first = id === \'group-1\';\\r\\n902:   return <>\\r\\n903:     <Section title={first ? \'Find the sufficient idea\' : \'Find what is required\'}>\\r\\n904:       <p>{first\\r\\n905:         ? \\"A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We\'ll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target.\\"\\r\\n"}'}]

See the vault asset\\r\\n// manifest and REVIEW.md for source IDs, raw originals and corrections.\\r\\nconst cards: Record<string, Card[]> = {\\r\\n  orientation: [\\r\\n    { question: \'What does a conditional statement tell you?\', answer: \'If one condition is true, the other has to be true too. The statement gives you that connection; it does not establish that either condition actually occurs. A matching fact can let you use the rule for a particular case.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 12 is marked reserved. What do we know?\', answer: \'Locker 12 has a name card. The rule covers every reserved locker, and the fact tells us Locker 12 belongs to that group. That matching condition is why the conclusion follows.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 9 has a name card. Is it necessarily marked reserved?\', answer: \'No. The sentence tells us what every reserved locker has. It has not restricted name cards to reserved lockers. Locker 9 could be reserved or unreserved; its name card alone leaves that question open.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Why do conditionals matter when evaluating an argument?\', answer: \'They help you check what you can conclude from the information you have. A rule might tell you that every reserved locker has a name card. That lets you infer a name card from a reservation. A name card alone does not prove a reservation; the rule never said only reserved lockers have them.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'sufficient-necessary\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'What does sufficient mean?\', answer: \'Enough to establish the other condition under this rule. Once the sufficient condition is met, the rule gives you the necessary condition. Enough does not mean it is the only possible way for the other condition to be true.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed. Does packing prove that it is marked ready?\', answer: \\"No. The rule tells us that an order marked ready for pickup has been packed. It doesn\'t say every packed order is marked ready. An order can be packed without that label, so packing alone doesn\'t settle whether it is marked ready.\\" },\\r\\n    { question: \'What does necessary mean?\', answer: \'Required whenever the sufficient condition holds. Ask required for what, and read the complete relationship. In Marked ready → Packed, packing is required for every ready order; those roles belong to this particular rule.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'If a requirement is met, does that prove the other condition happened?\', answer: \'No. A requirement may be present while the other condition remains unknown. Entry requires a pass, but a person could have a pass and stay outside. The rule has not turned the pass into a guarantee of entry.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'group-1\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'A student receives feedback if the student revises the essay. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Revises essay → Receives feedback. If refers to revising, even though that clause appears last. The sentence gives feedback to each student who revises. Feedback alone does not establish that the student revised.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. Why is “Applicant → Pays fee” too broad?\', answer: \'It drops the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would include someone who submitted on time, whom the sentence does not cover. Keep the complete rule: Applicant submits late → Pays fee.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Every student who registers early receives a discount. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Student registers early → Receives discount. Every refers to students who register early. Early registration is part of the sufficient condition, so keep it when you shorten the idea.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A fee is paid by any applicant who submits late. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Applicant submits late → Pays fee. Any still refers to late-submitting applicants. Moving that phrase later does not change its sufficient role. Read the arrow as: if an applicant submits late, that applicant pays a fee.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n  \'group-2\': [\\r\\n    { question: \'A permit is required for construction. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Construction → Has permit. What is required? The permit, even though it appears before required. The sentence makes it necessary for construction, not enough to establish that construction occurs.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'Why not automatically take the words immediately after the indicator?\', answer: \'The complete target can appear before or after the expression. In a permit is required for construction, the permit is the requirement. Ask what the indicator refers to, then check your diagram against the meaning of the whole sentence.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A finalist must attend the interview. Maya attended. Does that prove she is a finalist?\', answer: \'No. The rule gives finalists an obligation; it does not say only finalists attend. Actual attendance is also different from being required to attend. The fact about Maya does not establish finalist status.\' },\\r\\n    { question: \'A garden light switches on only when its sensor detects darkness. Write the rule.\', answer: \'Light switches on → Sensor detects darkness. Only when makes darkness detection required for switching on. The sentence has not said detection is enough, so knowing the sensor detects darkness alone would leave the light unknown.\' },\\r\\n  ],\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Recall({ items }: { items: Card[] }) {\\r\\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const answerId = useId();\\r\\n  const item = items[index];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-flash\\">\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Review · {index + 1} of {items.length}</p>\\r\\n    <div className={`co-flash-face ${revealed ? \'co-flipped\' : \'\'}`}>\\r\\n      <strong>{item.question}</strong><span>Answer in your own words, then compare.</span>\\r\\n      <div id={answerId} aria-live=\\"polite\\">{revealed && <p>{item.answer}</p>}</div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\">\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? \'Hide recall explanation\' : \'Reveal recall explanation\'}</button>\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setRevealed(false); }}>Previous example</button>\\r\\n      <button type=\\"button\\" onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setRevealed(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? \'Start these again\' : \'Next example\'}</button>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalOpeningMedia({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {\\r\\n  // Group lessons already have seven original sentence-to-rule recall cards.\\r\\n  // Their final meaning check closes the lesson without repeating a second deck.\\r\\n  if (unitId === \'group-1\' || unitId === \'group-2\') return null;\\r\\n  const items = cards[unitId];\\r\\n  if (!items) return null;\\r\\n  return <section id=\\"co-return-to-the-relationship\\" className=\\"co-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Return to the relationship\\">\\r\\n    <h2>Return to the relationship</h2>\\r\\n    <p>Try these before moving on. Packed alone does not establish the ready label. The drawing does not require unequal group sizes.</desc>\\r\\n            <rect x=\\"10\\" y=\\"10\\" width=\\"580\\" height=\\"230\\" rx=\\"24\\" fill=\\"#d7e9df\\"/>\\r\\n            <rect x=\\"40\\" y=\\"78\\" width=\\"285\\" height=\\"132\\" rx=\\"18\\" fill=\\"#fcf3df\\" stroke=\\"#bd8a47\\"/>\\r\\n          </svg>\\r\\n          <span className=\\"co-overview-packed-label\\">Packed orders</span><span className=\\"co-overview-ready-label\\">Orders marked ready</span>\\r\\n        </div>\\r\\n        <p>Packed alone: the ready label remains unknown.</p>\\r\\n        <figcaption>Read the arrow in its stated direction. An order could be packed without a ready label.</figcaption>\\r\\n      </figure>\\r\\n    </details>}\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': Distinguish imagining the if-condition from reporting that it occurred. | Read the rule; describe what would follow **if** its first condition were true. A brief meaning check comes before notation. |\\r\\n| **2. Sufficient: enough to establish the other condition** | Name the if-part. Explain that it may or may not be true. If it is absent, this rule alone does not settle the other condition. If it is present, the required condition follows. Define using or activating the rule through that present sufficient condition. | Compare the same rule with a sufficient-present fact and a sufficient-absent fact. Explain both conclusions before revealing the answers. |\\r\\n| **3. The sufficient word family** | Introduce the family in the source\'s row order: if; when/whenever; any/anytime; all; every/every time; in order to; people who; each. Show equivalent ways to express one relationship. Explain grouped forms and optional endings. Explain why understanding the sentence matters more than spotting a word. | Compare ordinary-English versions of a stable relationship; identify the complete sufficient idea. No arrow-construction task yet. Add current Group 1 expressions as clearly contextual extensions here. |\\r\\n| **4. Necessary: what has to be true** | Name the then-part. Explain its required role when the sufficient condition is true. Establish certainty about that requirement. Avoid implying that the condition must happen later in time. | In the same anchor, explain what is required and why. Keep the sufficient and necessary roles visible as complete ideas. |\\r\\n| **5. The necessary word family** | Follow the source\'s row order: then; must; necessary; required; only/only if; depends; need/need to; have to; essential; precondition. Show how these express the same relationship as the earlier family. Give only if special attention: it identifies a requirement despite containing if. Explain that symbols will help keep the relationship clear when wording changes. | Read and compare necessary-language sentences, including a controlled if versus only-if contrast. Add current Group 2 forms here without silently reclassifying Joshua\'s groups. |\\r\\n| **6. Discover the contrapositive** | Restate the sufficient-to-necessary relationship. Use a fresh thought experiment. Remove the necessary condition and ask whether the sufficient condition could still be true. Pause. Derive the answer from the unmet requirement. Name the contrapositive only after that reasoning, then return to the anchor. | Predict before reveal, explain the contradiction in ordinary English, and apply the reasoning to the anchor. Do not start with flip-and-negate. |\\r\\n| **7. Compare all four situations** | Teach in the source\'s chart order: sufficient present; necessary absent; sufficient absent; necessary present. The first establishes the necessary condition; the second rules out the sufficient condition; the other two leave the other condition unsettled by this rule alone. Explain the tempting reverse and inverse at this point. | Use a four-case interaction. State the strongest justified conclusion, then show an allowed case where an unsupported guess fails. Keep the rule available for later facts. |\\r\\n| **8. Write the relationship with an arrow** | Recover both word families. Introduce sufficient on the left, necessary on the right and the must-arrow between them. Explain implication, not chronology or causation. Build a first diagram in the source\'s construction order: notice the indicator; draw the arrow; abbreviate the complete target on its role side; fill the other condition. Explain each abbreviation. | Watch a narrated construction, then place complete ideas and read the result back in English. Sentence order can differ from diagram order. |\\r\\n| **9. Write and read the contrapositive** | Diagram the meaning already learned. Introduce the not-sign. Work through all four sign patterns: neither side negative, necessary negative, sufficient negative, both negative. Derive reverse-and-negate as a shortcut. Explain equivalence. Then distinguish writing the contrapositive from reading it when useful. | Build and read each form; explain why the transformed rule says the same thing. Teach Joshua\'s full five-step routine here: indicator; two complete ideas; role placement; contrapositive; English readback. |\\r\\n| **10. Answer practical diagramming questions** | Keep the source\'s FAQ order: when to diagram; how to check correctness and build confidence; concern about time; when to write a contrapositive; why training still asks for written contrapositives; how to choose readable abbreviations. | Compare a diagram with the original sentence. Diagnose a specific mistranslation and record what caused it. Start untimed; timing can be added to teacher-assigned medium/advanced practice. |\\r\\n| **11. Find the indicator\'s target** | Define target. Mark complete targets in familiar sentences. First show the usual position after an indicator. Then show targets before an indicator. Then show the same indicator moving relative to its target. The source\'s obscured line remains missing evidence, not a sentence to reconstruct. | Highlight the complete idea, including qualifications; compare meaning-preserving word-order variants. The closest noun or next word is not an automatic target rule. |\\r\\n| **12. Check with Personalized If/Then** | Define the translation check and why it helps. Read ordinary meaning; propose the sufficient condition; imagine a case meeting it; ask what must be true. Work a correct interpretation, then a deliberately reversed interpretation and explain why the latter fails. Finish by recommending the meaning check. | Perform both readings against the same whole sentence. Keep who or what the sentence concerns intact. Extend with Joshua\'s What Test as a complementary meaning check. |\\r\\n| **13. Practice the whole process** | Explain the drill\'s purpose, permit looking back, and require an original diagram and contrapositive. Begin with straightforward sufficient language, then varied sentence placement and necessary language. Use fresh original items and explain the target, roles, original, contrapositive and English readback. Provide both targeted family practice and a mixed transfer set. |\\r\\n\\r\\n### An original anchor for the rebuild\\r\\n\\r\\n**If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.**\\r\\n\\r\\n- Opening: imagine an order with the ready label. Packing follows under the stated rule; the sentence hasn\'t said an actual order has the label.\\r\\n- Sufficient: a ready label is enough to establish packing. Without the label, packing remains possible.\\r\\n- Necessary: packing is required for an order carrying that label.\\r\\n- Contrapositive: an order that hasn\'t been packed cannot be marked ready under this rule. Let the student work this out before naming it.\\r\\n- Four situations: ready implies packed; not packed implies not ready; not ready leaves packing open; packed leaves the ready label open.\\r\\n- Notation, introduced later: Ready → Packed; Not packed → Not ready. Abbreviate only after defining the complete labels.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis anchor is our own course example. Reuse it when a new view deepens the idea, and use fresh situations for transfer.
