Formatting has only been adjusted for readability and flow.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>The Art of the If/Then</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Conditional reasoning is the art of the <em>if/then</em>. If one thing happens, then another thing must happen. That’s it.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s explore our first conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Because you’re a thinking human, you can probably guess what this means at a literal level. If I’m breathing, there’s air around. The core of the relationship is that simple. If we have one thing, we <strong>must</strong> have the other thing. Just like with the Power Players, we have to take this <strong>MUST</strong> seriously. There is no way around this relationship. If you have the first thing, you have to have the second thing.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>For all the ballyhoo about conditional reasoning, you would think it’s a lot more complicated than that. The key to keeping conditional reasoning simple through its many applications, which we will examine in detail throughout this chapter, is understanding the concept behind it. I want you to intuitively understand the relationship at the heart of conditionals. Once you have that, the verbal idiosyncrasies that separate one conditional from another will be nothing more than blips on the radar.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s start by taking our conditional example apart piece by piece.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>The Sufficient Condition</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The “if” part of the conditional is called the sufficient condition.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The sufficient condition is a door opener. It’s welcoming. It could occur, but it doesn’t have to. Just like an open door, someone may walk in, but they don’t have to. In the example, I might be breathing or I might not: I could be holding my breath trying to get rid of hiccups or I could be breathing. We don’t know. We just know what will happen if I <em>am</em> breathing.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>If the sufficient condition is absent, you can completely ignore the conditional statement.</strong> If I’m not breathing, this conditional doesn’t matter at all. There might be air; there might not be air. The conditional wasn’t activated, so who cares? I’m not breathing, so it doesn’t matter.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>But if the sufficient condition is present, the conditional statement must be followed.</strong> If I am breathing, there absolutely 100% has to be air. The conditional statement gets activated and everything goes according to plan.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n  <strong>What does it mean to activate a conditional?</strong>\\r\\n  <p>It means the sufficient condition is present! So the conditional must be followed.</p>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The sufficient condition extends beyond just the word “if.” There is a whole family of words that work just like “if”; they let you know you have a sufficient condition on your hands.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here are the members of the sufficient family:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>Sufficient Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td>if</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>when(ever)</td><td><em>When I’m breathing, air is present.</em><br><br><em>Whenever I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>any(time)</td><td><em>Any breathing requires that air is present.</em><br><br><em>Anytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>all</td><td><em>All instances of breathing have air present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>every(time)</td><td><em>Every instance of breathing has air present.</em><br><br><em>Everytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>in order to</td><td><em>In order to breathe, I must have air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>people who</td><td><em>People who breathe need air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>each</td><td><em>Each instance of breathing has air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice how all of the sufficient family members have similar personalities. They are all inclusive, open words. Think back to the similarities between these words if/when you get lost in complex language.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>I had to smooth out some of the linguistic rough edges to translate that conditional into all the different versions, but the core relationship remained the same. <strong>Language is the challenge of conditionals; you have to distill the same conditional relationship from many different wording combinations.</strong> This is why it’s so important to understand the relationship on a deeper level than “look for indicator words.” The words are the hard part, so relying on indicators exclusively is a ticking time bomb.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n  <strong>What’s with all the parentheses? What does when(ever) mean?</strong>\\r\\n  <p>The parentheses are referring to an optional addition to the original word. Both “when” and “whenever” are members of the sufficient family. Conditionally, all of the examples in the chart above mean exactly the same thing as our initial example.</p>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>The Necessary Condition</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The “then” part of the conditional is called the necessary condition.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The necessary condition slams the door that the sufficient condition opens. It is the place of certainty. I mean just look at its name! It’s called the <em>necessary</em> condition because it is literally necessary. It has to occur at some point if the conditional relationship is activated. You can prove things that live in the necessary condition.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>There is a family of words that serves the same purpose as “then.” Each of these words lets you know you have a necessary condition in play. Here are the members of the necessary family:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>Necessary Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td>then</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>must</td><td><em>Air must be present for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>necessary</td><td><em>Air is necessary for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>required</td><td><em>Air is required for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>only (if)</td><td><em>Only the presence of air allows for breathing.</em><br><br><em>I’m breathing, only if air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>depends</td><td><em>Breathing depends on air being present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>need (to)</td><td><em>I need air to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>have to</td><td><em>I have to have air, if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>essential</td><td><em>Air is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>precondition</td><td><em>Air is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice the similarities in each of these words. They are all ironclad words. They are serious and restrictive; they are obligations.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\"><strong>Pay special attention to ONLY IF!</strong> It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>The Contrapositive</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>This relationship is ironclad. We’ll call it the <strong>Must Relationship</strong>. Remember this as we explore a conditional thought experiment.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Imagine you’re a coffee-obsessed individual. If you’re walking, you’re on your way to get a coffee. Getting a coffee is the only reason you ever walk anywhere. Every single time you walk, you are getting a coffee.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now, imagine that you are not on your way to get a coffee.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>What do you know about yourself?</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Put your guess here: ______________________________</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"stop\\">STOP</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Really try to figure this out right now. Don’t read the solution until you have a guess. I put this question to every student. The ones that hazard a guess (either voluntarily or under protest) always come away better off.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The fact that you are not on your way to get a coffee means you are not walking.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"remember\\">REMEMBER!</div>\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. <strong>The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized.</strong> If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s return to our original example to see how its contrapositive looks:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship.</strong> The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” <strong>The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Check out this chart showing what does and <em>does not</em> activate your conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead>\\r\\n    <tr><th>Situations</th><th>What Has to Happen?</th><th>If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.</th></tr>\\r\\n  </thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Present</strong></td><td><em>Must have necessary condition</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, so there must be air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><strong>Necessary Absent</strong></td><td><em>Can’t have sufficient condition</em></td><td><em>There’s no air, so I can’t breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Absent</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><strong>Necessary Present</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. <strong>Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>How to Diagram Conditionals</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>IF — Sufficient Condition</th><th>→</th><th>THEN — Necessary Condition</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr>\\r\\n      <td>\\r\\n        when(ever)<br>\\r\\n        any(time)<br>\\r\\n        all<br>\\r\\n        every(time)<br>\\r\\n        in order to<br>\\r\\n        people who<br>\\r\\n        each\\r\\n      </td>\\r\\n      <td>→</td>\\r\\n      <td>\\r\\n        must<br>\\r\\n        necessary<br>\\r\\n        required<br>\\r\\n        only (if)<br>\\r\\n        depends<br>\\r\\n        need (to)<br>\\r\\n        have to<br>\\r\\n        essential<br>\\r\\n        precondition\\r\\n      </td>\\r\\n    </tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>The Must Arrow</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The conditional relationship is ideal for symbolic representation because it is always the same underneath the varying language. We symbolize the conditional relationship with one simple sign:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">Sufficient  →  Necessary\\r\\n              ↑\\r\\n          Must Arrow</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>We use the Must Arrow to signify the Must Relationship. It is a reminder of the urgency of conditionals. Every conditional relationship can be written like this. We call it <em>diagramming the conditional</em>.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here are your steps for diagramming a conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<ol>\\r\\n  <li>See a conditional indicator.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Draw a Must Arrow.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Place an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.\\r\\n    <ul>\\r\\n      <li>Sufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.</li>\\r\\n      <li>Necessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.</li>\\r\\n    </ul>\\r\\n"}}'}]

\'@/components/ui/dynamic-island-toc\';\\r\\nimport type { ConditionalUnit } from \'../content/types\';\\r\\nimport { LessonInteraction } from \'./LessonInteractions\';\\r\\nimport \'../notes.css\';\\r\\nimport { ConditionalOpeningLesson, hasOpeningLesson } from \'./ConditionalOpeningLesson\';\\r\\n\\r\\nexport interface ConditionalLessonNoteProps {\\r\\n  unit: ConditionalUnit;\\r\\n  onComplete: () => void;\\r\\n  continueHref: string;\\r\\n  continueLabel?: string;\\r\\n  previousHref?: string;\\r\\n  reviewed: boolean;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction sectionTitle(unit: ConditionalUnit, index: number): string {\\r\\n  if (unit.id === \'orientation\') return [\'What a conditional says\', \'A rule needs a case fact\', \'Keep the strength of the claim\'][index] || \'Read the complete guarantee\';\\r\\n  if (unit.id === \'rules-facts\') return [\'Separate the fact and rule\', \'What each fact can establish\', \'A chain produces a new rule\', \'Show the match and explain why\', \'Four facts, one unchanged rule\', \'A requirement is not a completed action\'][index] || \'Check the whole claim\';\\r\\n  if (unit.area === \'translation\') return [\'Read the indicator in context\', \'Preserve the complete ideas\', \'Check the meaning before shortening\', \'Use a meaning check\', \'Practice the translation\', \'Recognize related expressions\', \'Keep the direction precise\'][index] || \'Compare the wording\';\\r\\n  if (unit.area === \'linking\') return [\'Find the exact connection\', \'Keep rules separate from facts\', \'Preserve subjects and scope\', \'Expose a hidden connection\', \'Make the path visible\', \'Check intermediate conclusions\', \'Match the complete requirement\'][index] || \'Test the proposed connection\';\\r\\n  return index === 0 ? \'Understand the relationship\' : index === 1 ? \'Compare the possible cases\' : index === 2 ? \'Keep the whole claim\' : `Check the reasoning ${index - 1}`;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function ConditionalLessonNote({ unit, onComplete, continueHref, continueLabel, previousHref, reviewed }: ConditionalLessonNoteProps) {\\r\\n  if (hasOpeningLesson(unit.id)) return <ConditionalOpeningLesson key={unit.id} unit={unit} onComplete={onComplete} continueHref={continueHref} continueLabel={continueLabel} previousHref={previousHref} reviewed={reviewed} />;\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"conditional-note\\">\\r\\n    <DynamicIslandTOC key={unit.id} selector=\\".conditional-note [data-toc]\\" />\\r\\n    <header className=\\"cn-hero\\"><div><p className=\\"cn-label\\">Conditional logic</p><h1>{unit.title}</h1><p>{unit.goal}</p></div><a className=\\"cn-start\\" href=\\"#conditional-note-start\\">Read the lesson<span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">↓</span></a></header>\\r\\n    <article id=\\"conditional-note-start\\" className=\\"cn-paper\\">\\r\\n      {unit.id === \'orientation\' && <section data-toc data-toc-title=\\"A promise about qualifying cases\\" className=\\"cn-section\\"><p className=\\"cn-label\\">Start with the meaning</p><h2>A promise about qualifying cases</h2><p>A conditional connects two complete ideas. It says: if the starting condition is met, the required result must follow. It does not say that the starting condition has already happened.</p><div className=\\"cn-diagram\\"><span>If this condition holds</span><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span><span>This result must follow</span></div><p>The left side is the trigger. The right side is what the rule guarantees. Understand that promise before memorizing indicator words.</p></section>}\\r\\n      {unit.teaching.map((paragraph, index) => <section key={index} className=\\"cn-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title={sectionTitle(unit, index)}><p className=\\"cn-label\\">{String(index + 1).padStart(2, \'0\')}</p><h2>{sectionTitle(unit, index)}</h2><p>{paragraph}</p></section>)}\\r\\n      <section className=\\"cn-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Work through an example\\"><h2>Work through an example</h2><div className=\\"cn-example\\">{unit.workedExample.statements.map((statement, index) => <p key={index}>{statement}</p>)}</div><ol className=\\"cn-reasoning\\">{unit.workedExample.reasoning.map((reason, index) => <li key={index}>{reason}</li>)}</ol><div className=\\"cn-result\\"><p className=\\"cn-label\\">What follows</p><p><strong>{unit.workedExample.conclusion}</strong></p></div></section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"cn-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Try the relationship\\"><h2>Try the relationship</h2><LessonInteraction unit={unit} /></section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"cn-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"A method to check your work\\"><h2>A method to check your work</h2><ol className=\\"cn-method\\">{unit.method.map((step, index) => <li key={index}>{step}</li>)}</ol></section>\\r\\n      <section id=\\"repair\\" className=\\"cn-section\\" data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Catch the tempting mistake\\"><h2>Catch the tempting mistake</h2><div className=\\"cn-contrast\\"><div><p className=\\"cn-label\\">The tempting move</p><p>{unit.commonMistake.mistake}</p></div><div><p className=\\"cn-label\\">Check it this way</p><p>{unit.commonMistake.repair}</p></div></div></section>\\r\\n      <footer className=\\"cn-footer\\"><p>{reviewed ? \'You marked this lesson as read. You can revisit it whenever you need.\' : \'Marking this lesson as read records review, not mastery.\'}</p><div className=\\"cn-controls\\">{previousHref && <Link to={previousHref}>Previous lesson</Link>}<button type=\\"button\\" onClick={onComplete}>{reviewed ? \'Lesson read\' : \'Mark lesson as read\'}</button><Link className=\\"cn-primary\\" to={continueHref}>Continue to practice →</Link></div></footer>\\r\\n    </article>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

number <= 7; number++) {\n+    expect(step(number).querySelector('.co-arrow'), `notation is premature in step ${number}`).toBeNull();\n+    expect(step(number).textContent).not.toContain('→');\n+  }\n+  expect(step(8).textContent).toMatch(/arrow|diagram/i);\n+  expect(step(9).textContent).toMatch(/contrapositive/i);\n+  expect(step(10).textContent).toMatch(/diagram|abbreviat/i);\n+  expect(step(11).textContent).toMatch(/target/i);\n+  expect(step(12).textContent).toMatch(/Personalized If\\/Then/);\n+  expect(step(13).textContent).toMatch(/practice|diagram/i);\n+});\n+\n+it('covers both source word families in their assigned teaching sections without a positional shortcut', () => {\n+  const { step } = mount();\n+  for (const expression of [\n+    /\\bif\\b/i, /\\bwhen\\b/i, /\\bwhenever\\b/i, /\\bany\\b/i, /\\banytime\\b/i,\n+    /\\ball\\b/i, /\\bevery\\b/i, /\\bevery time\\b/i, /\\bin order to\\b/i,\n+    /\\bpeople who\\b/i, /\\beach\\b/i,\n+  ]) expect(step(3).textContent, `sufficient family is missing ${expression}`).toMatch(expression);\n+  for (const expression of [\n+    /\\bthen\\b/i, /\\bmust\\b/i, /\\bnecessary\\b/i, /\\brequired\\b/i, /\\bonly\\b/i,\n+    /\\bonly if\\b/i, /\\bdepends\\b/i, /\\bneed\\b/i, /\\bneed to\\b/i,\n+    /\\bhave to\\b/i, /\\bessential\\b/i, /\\bprecondition\\b/i,\n+  ]) expect(step(5).textContent, `necessary family is missing ${expression}`).toMatch(expression);\n+  expect(step(3).textContent).toMatch(/in order to/i);\n+  expect(step(11).textContent).toMatch(/before/i);\n+});\n*** End Patch"));

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"05ead2","wall_time_seconds":3.2022021,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3693,"output":"  </li>\\r\\n  <li>Fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with whatever is left over in the statement.</li>\\r\\n</ol>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s take diagramming conditionals for a spin together. We’ll start with our favorite statement:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a <strong>B</strong> in the sufficient condition:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">B →</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with <strong>A</strong> for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">B → A</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>There you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">~A → ~B</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">Check out the little squiggle (~) in front of the ~A and ~B. We’ll use the squiggle throughout this text to negate variables. Read the squiggle as a “not.”</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Check out a few more contrapositive examples:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>Original Statement</th><th>Equivalent Contrapositive</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td>A → B</td><td>~B → ~A</td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>Q → ~R</td><td>R → ~Q</td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>~L → P</td><td>~P → L</td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>~Y → ~X</td><td>X → Y</td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>Once you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>I don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter</strong> just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. <strong>Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive.</strong> Both statements are describing the same relationship.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">\\r\\n  <p>Instead of writing the contrapositive out every time, you can just read the contrapositive when it’s relevant. Every conditional can be read two ways:</p>\\r\\n  <ol>\\r\\n    <li>Read it forwards from left to right.</li>\\r\\n    <li>Read it backwards from right to left, negating everything as you go (reading the contrapositive without having to write it out).</li>\\r\\n  </ol>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Awesome! Let’s talk through some frequent diagramming issues together.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"qa\\">\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>When should I diagram a conditional?</em></div>\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\r\\n    <p><strong>In the beginning, you should always diagram conditionals.</strong> This trains you to make sure you’re actually thinking conditionally. The language in LR is your biggest obstacle. Diagramming the conditional gets rid of all the linguistic ambiguity. You don’t have to look back at the text once you’re sure you diagrammed the conditional correctly. (I’ll get to how you’ll be sure in a second. Hold tight.)</p>\\r\\n    <p>When you are more experienced, you should diagram whenever the stimulus is not easy. I always err on the side of diagramming; I think of it as using the paper to my advantage. If I can take down a note to free myself of the exact words of the stimulus, I’m going to do it. I see far more problems from students declining to diagram than I do from students who diagram too much. If there is more than one conditional in a stimulus, you should diagram them to see if there is chaining potential.</p>\\r\\n    <p>Besides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>How will I ever be sure my conditional is correctly diagrammed?</em></div>\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\r\\n    <p>Seeing it work out correctly enough times breeds confidence. In your Wrong Answer Journal, keep track of every time you incorrectly diagram a conditional and make note of exactly how the language tricked you. Study these errors so you don’t make the same mistake again. <strong>Your mistakes are your greatest asset.</strong></p>\\r\\n    <p>The only way to ever be 100% sure that your diagram is right is confidence in your own abilities. There will never be a little green checkmark that spontaneously appears on the page to tell you that you’re right.</p>\\r\\n    <p>Everyone messes this up in the beginning. You can’t let that kill your confidence. Do the work, build skill, and you will eventually know that you’re right.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>But diagramming takes sooooo much time. I need to be fast. Do I really have to?</em></div>\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\r\\n    <p>Diagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.</p>\\r\\n    <p>Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>Should I always diagram the contrapositive?</em></div>\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\r\\n    <p>No. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.</p>\\r\\n    <p>That said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">You’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"qa\\">\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>What’s this abbreviation stuff?</em></div>\\r\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\r\\n    <p>When you diagram, you should shorten each condition to a capital letter or two. For example:</p>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m a chair, then I’m furniture.</p>\\r\\n    <p>We would shorten the “chair” to C and shorten the “furniture” to F. Ending up with this diagram:</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"diagram\\">C → F</div>\\r\\n    <p>For more complex conditions, just use the minimum number of letters that you will actually understand. Say one of the conditions is “maximum return on investment,” you should shorten that to MRI or MR… Whichever option calls up the condition in your mind will work. <strong>Don’t write out the whole word or more than four letters per condition.</strong> The notation becomes cumbersome when you write more than that.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>Figuring Out The Language</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>In this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>If [I’m breathing], then air is present.</em></td><td><em>Each [instance of breathing] has air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>When [I’m breathing], air is present.</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, only if [air is present].</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>Any [breathing] means air is present.</em></td><td><em>Only [the presence of air] allows for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>All [instances of breathing] have air present.</em></td><td><em>Breathing depends on [air being present].</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>Every [instance of breathing] has air present.</em></td><td><em>I need [air] to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>In order to [breathe], I must have air.</em></td><td><em>I have to have [air], if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>People who [breathe] need air.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] must be present for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is necessary for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is required for breathing.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>In these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is <strong>really saying</strong>. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] must be present for breathing. / There must be [air] for us to breathe.</p>\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].</p>\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is required for breathing. / I require [air] to breathe.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>[Final line at the bottom of the image is partially obscured.]</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here’s another safeguard on conditional diagramming. <strong>Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation.</strong> This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>How to Create a Personalized If/Then</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Imagine you’re confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<ol>\\r\\n  <li>Try to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning of the sentence.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Put yourself in your guess’ situation and ask what you know about yourself.</li>\\r\\n  <li>If your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.</li>\\r\\n</ol>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s translate an example into a personalized if/then:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">Orchids depend on frequent conversation.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Before you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">The key to intuitively understanding conditionals is personalizing them. Always put yourself in the shoes of the conditional, no matter what it’s about.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So, if I’m an orchid, what do I know about me? I know that I have to have frequent conversation. I depend on frequent conversation. If I’m an orchid, <em>then</em> I have frequent conversation. This fits. It is the correct translation of the conditional.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t <em>need</em> anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The personalized if/then is how I always do conditionals, unless they are already phrased as if/thens.</strong> It gives me confidence throughout the test that I’m not making a mistake.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>Basic Conditional Drill — Much Ado About Nothing</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The conditional drills in this chapter are each inspired by Shakespeare plays, which you will see identified in the title of each drill. The lines are very lightly edited to highlight their conditionality, but these are more-or-less the real lines from Shakespeare. And yes, Shakespeare is really this outlandish; high school didn’t do him justice.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>Instructions</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><em>Diagram the following conditional statements. Don’t be afraid to look back at the section, if you need any help.</em></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<ol>\\r\\n  <li>\\r\\n"}'}]

P = has been packed.\', original: \'R → P\', options: [\'P → R\', \'~P → ~R\', \'~R → ~P\'], correct: 1, answer: \'~P → ~R\', reading: \'If an order has not been packed, it is not marked ready for pickup.\', mistake: \'Packing by itself leaves the ready label open, and the missing label leaves packing open. If the required unlocked condition is absent, the not-closed condition cannot hold.\' },\\r\\n  { label: \'Both sides negative\', sentence: \'If an item is not marked fragile, it is not on the glass shelf.\', legend: \'F = item is marked fragile. G = item is on the glass shelf.\', original: \'~F → ~G\', options: [\'~G → ~F\', \'G → F\', \'F → G\'], correct: 1, answer: \'G → F\', reading: \'If an item is on the glass shelf, it is marked fragile.\', mistake: \'Being off the shelf does not establish a missing fragile mark, and having that mark does not establish shelf placement. The two existing not-signs both disappear when you negate their conditions.\' },\\r\\n];\\r\\nfunction SignPatterns() {\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-pattern-grid\\">{signPatterns.map((item, index) => <div className=\\"cr-pattern\\" key={item.label}><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">{index + 1}. {item.label}</p><p>{item.sentence}</p><p className=\\"cr-legend\\">{item.legend}</p><div className=\\"cr-formula\\">{item.original}</div><ReferenceQuestion prompt={`Which is the contrapositive of ${item.original}?`} options={item.options} correct={item.correct} feedback={item.options.map((_, option) => option === item.correct ? `${item.reading} This is the same rule: the missing necessary condition rules out the sufficient one.` : item.mistake)}/></div>)}</div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\ntype TargetExample = { label: string; before: string; target: string; after: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] };\\r\\nconst targetExamples: TargetExample[] = [\\r\\n  { label: \'Target after the indicator\', before: \'Only \', target: \'reports with editor approval\', after: \' are published.\', options: [\'reports\', \'reports with editor approval\', \'published\'], correct: 1, feedback: [\'Reports alone drops with editor approval. The statement restricts publication to that qualified group.\', \'Only refers to reports with editor approval. Keep that qualification; it is the required idea.\', \'Published is the other condition. Only limits which reports can meet it.\'] },\\r\\n  { label: \'Target before the indicator\', before: \'\', target: \'Editor approval\', after: \' is necessary for publication.\', options: [\'publication\', \'editor approval\'], correct: 1, feedback: [\'Publication is what has the requirement. Editor approval is what the sentence calls necessary.\', \'The target comes before necessary. Editor approval is the requirement for publication.\'] },\\r\\n  { label: \'The same word can move\', before: \'Publication makes it necessary to have \', target: \'editor approval\', after: \'.\', options: [\'publication\', \'editor approval\'], correct: 1, feedback: [\'Publication still needs something. Moving necessary earlier has not made publication the requirement.\', \'Editor approval is still what publication requires. Here the target comes after necessary; in the previous sentence it came before.\'] },\\r\\n];\\r\\nfunction TargetReader({ item }: { item: TargetExample }) {\\r\\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<number | null>(null);\\r\\n  const correct = choice === item.correct;\\r\\n  const id = useId();\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-target co-lab\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">{item.label}</p><blockquote>{item.before}{correct ? <mark>{item.target}</mark> : item.target}{item.after}</blockquote><h3 id={id}>Which complete idea does the indicator refer to?</h3><div className=\\"cr-answer-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-labelledby={id}>{item.options.map((option, index) => <button key={option} type=\\"button\\" aria-pressed={choice === index} onClick={() => setChoice(index)}>{option}</button>)}</div><div role=\\"status\\">{ch…2668 tokens truncated…ey. We will express that same connection in several ways. We haven\'t said everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n      <WordFamily entries={sufficientFamily} role=\\"sufficient\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>When/whenever and any/anytime group related forms together. An ending changes the kind of case being discussed, but these examples keep the same connection. Read the whole sentence. A familiar word is a clue, not a substitute for its meaning.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Every guest wearing a blue badge receives a lunch voucher. Which complete idea is enough?\\" options={[\'Guest\', \'Guest wearing a blue badge\', \'Receives a lunch voucher\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Guest alone drops the badge restriction and would make a claim about unbadged guests.\', \'The rule gives a voucher to every guest wearing a blue badge. Keep that qualification with the person.\', \'The voucher is what the rule establishes. It has not said every voucher recipient wears a blue badge.\']}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension\\"><summary>Two more Group 1 expressions</summary><p><strong>Where</strong> can state a relationship across locations: “Where a collection point operates, parcels can be returned.” An operating collection point establishes the availability of returns there. Availability is not proof that someone made a return.</p><p><strong>The only</strong> needs its complete phrase: “The only notebooks on this shelf are hardback.” A notebook on the shelf is hardback. The sentence does not put every hardback notebook there.</p><p>These are part of your Group 1 work. Understand what the full expression covers before using its group as a placement shortcut.</p></details>\\r\\n      <p>We have seen what can be enough. Now let\'s name what that condition requires.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={4} label=\\"Necessary means required\\" title=\\"The other condition has to be there\\">\\r\\n      <blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote><p>The <strong>then-part</strong> is the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. A ready order has to have been packed.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-role-exhibit cr-required\\"><span>Necessary</span><strong>Has been packed</strong><p>Required for an order marked ready</p></div>\\r\\n      <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order with the ready label. These names describe the roles the conditions have in this relationship.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'re looking at the same connection from its other side. The ready label gives us enough to establish packing. Packing is what every ready order requires.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Under our rule, which condition must a ready order meet?\\" options={[\'It has been packed.\', \'It has been collected by the customer.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Packing is the stated requirement. A ready order that was not packed would violate the rule.\', \'Collection is not part of this rule. The sentence establishes packing, not whether the customer collected the order.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Necessary does not mean “the next event.” It means required by this relationship. A conditional need not tell you which event caused another, or when either happened.</p>\\r\\n      <p>As with sufficient language, the wording can vary while the relationship stays the same.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={5} label=\\"The necessary family\\" title=\\"Listen for what the sentence needs\\">\\r\\n      <p>Return to our locker rule. Borrowing a locker requires a key. The next expressions all identify that requirement in their complete sentences.</p>\\r\\n      <WordFamily entries={necessaryFamily} role=\\"necessary\\"/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-contrast\\"><div><h3>If</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is enough for borrowing under this sentence.</p></div><div><h3>Only if</h3><p>“You borrow a locker <strong>only if</strong> you have a key.”</p><p>Having a key is required. This sentence has not made it enough.</p></div></div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A guest enters the lounge only if the guest has a current pass. What does entering establish?\\" options={[\'The guest has a current pass.\', \'Every guest with a pass enters.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Only if identifies the current pass as required for entry. An entering guest therefore meets that requirement.\', \'You made the requirement enough. A guest could have a current pass and remain outside without violating the sentence.\']}/>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension\\"><summary>More Group 2 forms, with the sentence intact</summary><p><strong>Only when</strong> and <strong>only where</strong> can restrict the occasions or places of an outcome: “The fountain runs only when its pump is on.” A running fountain requires an operating pump. The pump being on does not establish that the fountain runs.</p><p><strong>Requires</strong> directly names a requirement: “Entry requires a current pass.” Preserve current. <strong>Always</strong> needs the sentence\'s subject and scope: “The upstairs room is always quiet when the door is shut.” Here a shut door establishes quiet, not the other way around.</p><p><strong>Only</strong> and <strong>the only</strong> are different complete expressions. “Only packed orders are marked ready” and “The only orders marked ready are packed” both tell us that a ready order is packed.</p></details>\\r\\n      <p>We can now recognize both roles before writing any symbols. Next, let\'s see what happens when a requirement is missing.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={6} label=\\"Discover the contrapositive\\" title=\\"What if the requirement is missing?\\" dark>\\r\\n      <p>Use this stated rule for a different case.</p><blockquote>If a report is in the published archive, it has an approval stamp.</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>A report in that archive has to carry the stamp. Now you inspect Report K. It has no approval stamp.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Could Report K be in the published archive while this rule holds?\\" options={[\'Yes, its location is unrestricted.\', \'No, it cannot be in that archive.\', \'Yes, the missing stamp puts it in the archive.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Its location is restricted by the rule. If K were in the archive, it would have a stamp, which conflicts with the given fact.\', \'An archived K would have to have a stamp. K has no stamp, so it cannot be in the published archive under this rule.\', \'The missing stamp rules out archive membership. It does not establish it.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That way of using the rule is the <strong>contrapositive</strong>: when the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition cannot be present.</p>\\r\\n      <p>It is the same relationship. We didn\'t add a second rule; we worked out a consequence already contained in the first one.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{orderRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"An order has not been packed. What follows about its ready label?\\" options={[\'It is not marked ready for pickup.\', \'It is marked ready for pickup.\', \'Nothing follows about the ready label.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'A ready label would require packing. Since packing is absent, the order cannot have that label under the rule.\', \'A ready but unpacked order would break the rule. The absent requirement excludes the ready label.\', \'The missing requirement does give an inference. If this order were ready, it would have to be packed.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Don\'t confuse that with starting from a requirement that is present. A packed order could still be waiting for its ready label. Let\'s compare all four starting facts.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={7} label=\\"The four situations\\" title=\\"Keep what follows separate from what stays open\\">\\r\\n      <p>Change the fact about one order while keeping the rule. Each fact gives a different starting point. Decide what it establishes before comparing the explanation.</p>\\r\\n      <FourCaseExplorer/>\\r\\n      <p>Starting from packing and concluding ready is the tempting <strong>reverse</strong>. Starting from not ready and concluding not packed is the tempting <strong>inverse</strong>. Neither follows from this rule alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive starts from <strong>not packed</strong> and establishes <strong>not ready</strong>. Its reasoning depends on the missing requirement, not on reversing whichever words happen to appear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We now understand the relationship in words. Symbols will let us record that understanding without carrying every word of the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\\"Build the first diagram\\" title=\\"Give the relationship a shorter form\\">\\r\\n      <p>Put the sufficient condition on the <strong>left</strong>, the necessary condition on the <strong>right</strong>, and a <strong>must arrow</strong> between them.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Sufficient: enough\\" right=\\"Necessary: required\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>The arrow means the left condition requires the right. It does not mean the left caused the right, or that the right happens later.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Both word families help us choose a condition\'s role. “If” can introduce what goes left. “Only if” can introduce what goes right. Sentence position alone does not choose the side.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\\r\\n      <IncrementalWriter/>\\r\\n      <p>Now move the if-clause: “An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup.” The ready condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job hasn\'t changed.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\\" options={[\'Packed → Ready\', \'Ready → Packed\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes being marked ready enough for packing.\', \'The sufficient condition is still marked ready. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.\']}/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\\"Write and read the contrapositive\\" title=\\"Write the meaning you already understand\\">\\r\\n      <p>Ready means the order is marked ready for pickup. Packed means it has been packed. Our original is Ready → Packed.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We already know that a missing packing requirement rules out the ready label. Write that as <strong>~Packed → ~Ready</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <p>To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate <strong>both complete conditions</strong>. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let\'s try all four patterns.</p>\\r\\n      <SignPatterns/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-method\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\\r\\n      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn\'t copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition\'s absence and work back to the left condition\'s absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\\"Practical diagramming questions\\" title=\\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-faq\\">\\r\\n        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. Your teacher can add timing to medium or advanced practice after the reasoning is stable.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-training-note\\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We\'re checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. R might mean marked ready for pickup. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you\'re tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\\" options={[\'The labels need to be longer.\', \'The arrow makes the requirement enough.\', \'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.\', \'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.\', \'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That error came from the language. Let\'s inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\\"Find the target\\" title=\\"Read the whole idea the indicator refers to\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Read which complete idea the sentence calls required. Then give that idea its role in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\\"Check an interpretation\\" title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-personalized\\"><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\\" options={[\'I am wearing a helmet.\', \'The helmet question is open.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.\', \'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.\']}/></div><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\\" options={[\'Yes, the helmet proves riding.\', \'No, I could wear one without riding.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.\', \'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.\']}/></div></div>\\r\\n      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Ask a sensible question about the expression: only what? People wearing helmets. What is required? Wearing a helmet. What is enough? Riding a studio bike, under this rule, establishes that requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The What Test finds the complete target. The Personalized If/Then checks what your proposed direction says about a case. Use them together when one part of the translation is unclear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ve now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\\"Use the whole process\\" title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-integrated-grid\\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]
